Table of Contents
Warrants Issued to Customers
Warrants to purchase the Company's common stock issued to customers represent consideration payable to a customer under ASC 606, Revenue from Contracts with Customers. Fair value on the grant date of the warrant shares probable of vesting is recorded as a reduction to revenue proportionally as qualifying customer revenues are recorded, initially with a corresponding liability recorded within Other long-term liabilities on the Condensed Consolidated Balance Sheets. The warrants are measured and classified under ASC 718, Compensation – Stock Compensation. The warrant liability is remeasured to fair value each reporting period, with changes in fair value after grant date recorded within Other income (expense), net, until each tranche becomes exercisable, at which point the related liability is reclassified to Additional paid-in capital at its then-current fair value and is no longer subject to remeasurement. If the conditions of exercisability are met prior to the customers meeting the tranche's purchase milestones, the unrecognized change in fair value is recorded within Other income (expense) with a corresponding amount recorded to Additional paid in capital as the customer purchases occur. The Company estimates the fair value using a Monte Carlo simulation model or, after the market conditions are met, the Company’s closing stock price, together with its estimate of the probability of the customers meeting the specific conditions required for exercisability. Once warrant shares become vested and exercisable, they are included in the computation of both basic and diluted earnings per share.
Prior Period Reclassification. Certain amounts in prior period were reclassified to conform to current period presentation.
Recently Issued Accounting Standard Updates Not Yet Adopted
In December 2025, the Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB) issued Accounting Standard Update (ASU) Government Grants (ASU 2025-10) Topic 832 - Accounting for Government Grants Received by Business Entities, which provides guidance, on the recognition, measurement and presentation of government grants. This ASU is effective for the Company for its fiscal year 2029. The Company is currently evaluating the impact of adoption of this guidance on its financial statements.
In May 2025, the FASB issued ASU 2025-04 Topic 718 - Stock Compensation and Topic 606 - Clarifications to Share-Based Consideration Payable to a Customer to clarify the guidance on accounting for share-based payment grants that an entity grants as consideration payable to its customers. The ASU is effective for the Company for its fiscal year 2027. The Company is currently evaluating the impact of adoption of this guidance on its financial statements.

NOTE 4 – Segment Reporting
Management, including the Chief Operating Decision Maker (CODM), who is the Company’s Chief Executive Officer, reviews and assesses operating performance using segment net revenue, cost of sales and operating expenses, and operating income (loss). These performance measures include the allocation of expenses to the reportable segments based on management’s judgment. The CODM is regularly provided segment operating income to assess relative segment performance.
The Company’s three reportable segments are:
•the Data Center segment, which primarily includes Artificial Intelligence (AI) accelerators, microprocessors (CPUs) for servers, graphics processing units (GPUs), accelerated processing units (APUs), data processing units (DPUs), AI Network Interface Cards (AI NICs), Field Programmable Gate Arrays (FPGAs) and adaptive System-on-Chip (SoC) products for data centers;
•the Client and Gaming segment, which primarily includes CPUs, APUs, chipsets for desktops and notebooks, discrete GPUs, and semi-custom SoC products and development services; and
•the Embedded segment, which primarily includes embedded CPUs, APUs, FPGAs, System on Modules (SOMs), and adaptive SoC products.
11
Table of Contents
From time to time, the Company may also sell or license portions of its IP portfolio.
In addition to these reportable segments, the Company has an All Other category, which is not a reportable segment. This category primarily includes certain expenses and credits that are not allocated to any of the reportable segments because the CODM does not consider these expenses and credits in evaluating the performance of the reportable segments. This category primarily includes amortization of acquisition-related intangibles, employee stock-based compensation expense and acquisition-related and other costs. Acquisition-related and other costs primarily include certain compensation charges and transaction costs.

Each of the Client and Gaming businesses do not qualify as a separate reportable operating segment, however, the Company continues to separately disclose revenue for each business.

NOTE 5 – Acquisitions and Divestitures
Pending Acquisitions
In August 2026, the Company entered into a definitive agreement to acquire Taalas Inc., a developer of specialized inference silicon, in a cash and stock transaction valued at approximately $2.9 billion, including contingent consideration of up to approximately $1.4 billion. In September 2026, the Company entered into a definitive agreement to acquire World Labs Technologies, Inc., a model research lab focused on spatial intelligence, in an all-stock transaction valued at approximately $8.2 billion. The acquisitions are expected to close by the end of 2026, subject to regulatory approvals and customary closing conditions.
ZT Systems Acquisition and ZT Manufacturing Business Divestiture
In March 2025, the Company completed the acquisition of ZT Group Int’l, Inc. (ZT Systems), a provider of data center and general-purpose compute infrastructure for hyperscale computing companies, for total purchase consideration of $4.4 billion, consisting of $3.2 billion in cash, $860 million in shares of AMD common stock and $361 million of contingent consideration. The contingent consideration was settled in October 2025 upon full satisfaction of the applicable contingencies. The acquisition supports the Company’s ability to deliver end-to-end AI solutions and accelerate the design and deployment of AMD-powered super intelligence (SI) infrastructure at scale optimized for the cloud.
The Company retained select intellectual property and employees associated with the design operations, the results of which are included in the Data Center segment within continuing operations and are not material. Goodwill, assigned to the Data Center reporting unit and primarily attributed to assembled workforce, is not deductible for income tax purposes. The data center infrastructure manufacturing business (ZT Manufacturing Business) was classified as held for sale upon acquisition and was divested in October 2025, and its results are presented as discontinued operations.
During the measurement period, the Company recorded adjustments to certain assets and liabilities acquired and classified as held for sale, with a corresponding adjustment to goodwill. The adjustments did not have a material impact on the Company’s consolidated results of operations. The purchase price allocation, including measurement period adjustments, is presented below.

Upon close of the sale, the Company received cash of $1.4 billion, net of cash divested and purchase price adjustments, and shares of Sanmina common stock valued at $154 million. NOTE 7 – Equity Method Investments
The Company has investments accounted for under the equity method, which are considered related parties. The carrying value of the Company’s investments accounted for under the equity method was $306 million and $176 million as of September 26, 2026 and December 27, 2025, respectively, and is recorded within Other non-current assets on the Company’s Consolidated Balance Sheets.
The Company’s transactions with these related parties consist primarily of assembly, test, mark and packaging (ATMP) services provided in the ordinary course of business. Purchases from related parties during the three and nine months ended September 26, 2026 were $581 million and $1.8 billion, respectively, and during the three and nine months ended September 27, 2025 were $483 million and $1.5 billion, respectively. Amounts payable to related parties were $606 million and $408 million as of September 26, 2026 and December 27, 2025, respectively, and are recorded within Accounts payable on the Company’s Consolidated Balance Sheets. The Company also has a $100 million term loan receivable outstanding from one of the investees as of September 26, 2026 and December 27, 2025, bearing interest at a variable rate plus a margin payable quarterly, recorded with related interest receivable within Prepaid expenses and other current assets on the Company’s Consolidated Balance Sheets.
Equity income from these investees, recorded in Equity income in investee on the Company’s Consolidated Statements of Operations, was not material for the three and nine months ended September 26, 2026 and September 27, 2025.

Financial Instruments Measured at Fair Value on a Non-Recurring Basis
Non-marketable equity securities and other instruments, which primarily consist of investments in privately held SI and emerging technology companies, are accounted for under the measurement alternative and are measured at fair value on a non-recurring basis when there is an observable price change or impairment. These fair value measurements are classified within Level 3 of the fair value hierarchy and are determined using valuation models that incorporate unobservable inputs. Certain of the Company’s investments in privately held cloud and SI infrastructure companies are variable interests in variable interest entities (VIEs). See Note 12 – Variable Interest Entities.
As of September 26, 2026 and December 27, 2025, the carrying value of these investments was $1.9 billion and $1.1 billion, respectively, which included cumulative gross unrealized gains of $145 million and $291 million, respectively. Gross unrealized gains were $155 million and $107 million during the nine months ended September 26, 2026 and September 27, 2025, respectively, and were not material during the three-month periods. Gross unrealized losses and impairments for all periods presented, including on a cumulative basis, were not material.
Hedging Transactions and Derivative Financial Instruments
Foreign Currency Forward Contracts Designated as Accounting Hedges
The Company enters into foreign currency forward contracts to hedge its exposure to foreign currency exchange rate risk related to future forecasted transactions denominated in currencies other than the U.S. Dollar. These contracts generally mature within 24 months and are designated as accounting hedges. As of September 26, 2026 and December 27, 2025, the notional value of the Company’s outstanding foreign currency forward contracts designated as cash flow hedges was $3.2 billion and $2.3 billion, respectively. The fair value of these contracts, on a gross basis, was not material as of September 26, 2026 and December 27, 2025.
Foreign Currency Forward Contracts Not Designated as Accounting Hedges
The Company also enters into foreign currency forward contracts to reduce the short-term effects of foreign currency fluctuations on certain receivables or payables denominated in currencies other than the U.S. Dollar. These forward contracts generally mature within 3 months and are not designated as accounting hedges. As of September 26, 2026 and December 27, 2025, the notional value of these outstanding contracts was $3.0 billion and $1.1 billion, respectively. The fair value of these contracts, on a gross basis, was not material as of September 26, 2026 and December 27, 2025.
Credit Derivatives
The Company has entered into guarantees to provide credit support for payment obligations of certain commercial partners, in the event of their default, to support their development of data center infrastructure. These guarantees, which include data center lease guarantees and commercial partner debt guarantees, are accounted for as credit derivatives and recorded at fair value on a recurring basis within Other current liabilities and Other long-term liabilities, with changes in fair value recognized in Other income (expense), net. The fair value of these credit derivatives is classified within Level 3 of the fair value hierarchy and is determined using valuation models that incorporate unobservable inputs, including, where applicable, lease rates, obligor credit ratings, credit spreads and default rates that were based on estimates applicable to obligors with comparable credit risk profiles. As of September 26, 2026, no commercial partner was in default and no amounts had been paid under these guarantees.
Data center lease guarantees. These guarantees cover certain commercial partners' data center lease obligations to third-party landlords. As of September 26, 2026, the maximum gross exposure was $7.7 billion over remaining terms of up to 15 years, and decreases as commercial partners satisfy their payment obligations. Upon a commercial partner’s default, the Company may assume the underlying leases for internal use, or assign or sublease to third parties. The guarantees may terminate upon the occurrence of certain events. As of September 26, 2026, the fair value of these data center lease guarantees, and the related gains and losses for the three and nine months ended September 26, 2026, were not material to the financial statements.
17
Table of Contents
Commercial partner debt guarantees. These guarantees cover certain commercial partners' borrowings used to finance data center infrastructure. As of September 26, 2026, the maximum gross exposure was $750 million with remaining terms of up to 6 months. Upon a commercial partner’s default, the Company will be required to repay the related borrowings, and may receive recovery from the commercial partner or the underlying data center assets. As of September 26, 2026, the fair value of these debt guarantees, and the related gains and losses for the three and nine months ended September 26, 2026, were not material to the financial statements.

Senior Notes
The Company repaid in full the $875 million aggregate principal amount of its 4.212% Senior Notes as the notes matured in September 2026.
The Company's senior notes are general unsecured senior obligations with semi-annual fixed interest payments. On August 17, 2026, the Company issued $4.75 billion aggregate principal amount of senior notes consisting of $1.25 billion of 4.600% Notes, $1.50 billion of 5.000% Notes, $1.00 billion of 5.250% Notes and $1.00 billion of 5.500% Notes (collectively, the August 2026 Notes), payable semi-annually on February 17 and August 17 of each year, commencing February 17, 2027. The 4.319% Notes pay interest on March 24 and September 24 of each year. The 2.375% Notes, 3.924% Notes and 4.393% Notes pay interest on June 1 and December 1 of each year.
Each series of the Company's senior notes is redeemable at the Company's option prior to maturity, subject to certain conditions, including payment of a make-whole premium. Holders of all series except the 2.375% Notes have the right to require the Company to repurchase their notes at 101% of the principal amount plus accrued and unpaid interest upon a change of control. An event of default may accelerate the maturity of any series. As of September 26, 2026, the Company was in compliance with the covenants associated with all of its notes.
18
Table of Contents
Revolving Credit Facility
The Company entered into a five-year, $5.0 billion unsecured revolving credit facility (the Revolving Facility) in May 2026 to replace its prior $3.0 billion facility. Subject to certain conditions, the Company may borrow, repay and reborrow under the Revolving Facility, with up to $250 million available for letters of credit, which reduces the aggregate amount otherwise available for revolving loans. Borrowings bear interest at variable market rates plus an applicable margin based on the Company's credit ratings. The Company pays a commitment fee on unused commitments. As of September 26, 2026 and December 27, 2025, the Company had no outstanding balance under the Revolving Facility and was in compliance with all associated covenants.
Commercial Paper
The Company has a commercial paper program under which it can issue unsecured commercial paper notes. In May 2026, the Company increased to $5.5 billion, from $3.0 billion, the maximum aggregate amount outstanding at any time of unsecured commercial paper notes which the Company may issue on a private placement basis. The Company can issue unsecured commercial paper notes at any time with maturities of up to 397 days from the date of issue. The commercial paper will be sold at a discount from par or, alternatively, will be sold at par and bear interest at rates that will vary based on market conditions at the time of the issuance. As of September 26, 2026 and December 27, 2025, the Company had no commercial paper outstanding.
NOTE 10 – Leases
The Company’s leases consist primarily of operating leases of data centers, research and development facilities and equipment that expire through fiscal year 2038. These include leases for general corporate purposes and data centers, including data center leases related to the development of SI infrastructure that have been subleased or are expected to be assigned or subleased to commercial partners.
Total net lease expense, which includes variable lease expense, was $112 million and $280 million for the three and nine months ended September 26, 2026, respectively, and $78 million and $217 million for the three and nine months ended September 27, 2025, respectively. Variable lease expense, primarily operating expenses and property taxes for leased facilities, was not material. Certain leases contain payment escalations based on changes in the consumer price index. The Company’s finance and short-term leases are not material.
For the nine months ended September 26, 2026 and September 27, 2025, cash paid for operating leases included in operating cash flows was $189 million and $131 million, respectively.

NOTE 12 - Variable Interest Entities
The Company holds various forms of interests in VIEs, including investments in certain privately held cloud and SI infrastructure companies, credit derivatives, commitments to purchase cloud compute capacity from certain CSPs, and subleases of certain data center facilities to commercial partners. The Company has determined that it does not have the power to direct the activities that most significantly impact the economic performance of these entities. Therefore, the Company is not the primary beneficiary, and these VIEs are not consolidated within its financial statements.
The Company’s investments in VIEs consist of non-marketable equity securities and are recorded within Other non-current assets, and the maximum exposure is limited to their carrying value. The Company has provided certain guarantees to VIEs that are accounted for as credit derivatives. The Company has interests in the forms of subleases of certain data center facilities to commercial partners that are VIEs. In addition, the Company entered into certain cloud compute capacity commitments with CSPs that are VIEs. See Note 8 – Financial Instruments, Note 10 – Leases and Note 11 – Commitments and Contingencies for further information on non-marketable equity investments, guarantees, leases and commitments.

Treasury Stock
During the three and nine months ended September 26, 2026, the Company retired 3.5 million and 70.4 million of treasury shares, respectively, which returned to the status of authorized but unissued shares. The excess of purchase price over par value was allocated between additional paid-in capital and retained earnings.
Stock Repurchase Program
The Company has a stock repurchase program with total repurchase authority of $14 billion. During the nine months ended September 26, 2026, the Company repurchased 1.1 million shares of its common stock for $221 million, excluding the 1% excise tax on stock repurchases under the Inflation Reduction Act of 2022. As of September 26, 2026, $9.2 billion remained available for future stock repurchases. The program does not obligate the Company to acquire any common stock, has no termination date and may be suspended or discontinued at any time.
Warrants Issued to Customers
The Company issued warrants to two customers in October 2025 and February 2026, each entitling the holder the right to purchase up to 160 million shares of the Company’s common stock at an exercise price of $0.01 per share. The warrants vest in tranches as the customers reach specified purchase milestones and the Company’s stock price meets specified targets, including, for certain tranches, stock price performance relative to a market index. Each tranche becomes exercisable only after specified technical and commercial conditions are met by the customers. Each warrant expires five years from its issuance date, and is not settleable in cash. Upon exercise, the Company will issue shares of common stock.
Fair value is based on the estimated probability of the customers meeting the conditions for exercisability and on market-based assumptions. The weighted-average grant-date fair value of warrants outstanding was $14.25 per share. Grant-date fair values were estimated using a Monte Carlo simulation with the following assumptions:
Expected volatility | 49% - 56%
Risk-free interest rate | 3.56% - 3.69%
Expected dividends | —%
Expected terms (in years) | 5
Expected volatility was based on historical and implied volatility of the Company's common stock, the risk-free interest rate was based on U.S. Treasury yields and the expected term was based on the contractual term of the warrants. For certain tranches, the Monte Carlo simulation also incorporated the historical correlation of the Company's stock price returns with a market index. As of September 26, 2026, the weighted-average fair value of the warrants was $178.58 per share, based on the Company’s updated probability of the customers meeting the conditions required for exercisability and primarily on its closing stock price as most market conditions had been met.
As of September 26, 2026, the Company had a warrant liability of $1.2 billion, representing the fair value of the warrants attributable to qualifying customer revenue recorded to date. During the three and nine months ended September 26, 2026, the Company recorded a noncash charge of $1.2 billion, primarily related to a loss from remeasurement of the warrant liability based on the change in fair value of the warrants within Other income (expense), net. The unrecognized grant-date fair value of the unvested warrants was $4.5 billion, representing the remaining value that would be recorded as a reduction of revenue proportionally as qualifying customer revenue is recognized. As of September 26, 2026, no warrant shares had vested or become exercisable, or been exercised, forfeited or expired. No warrant liability had been reclassified to Additional paid-in capital.

NOTE 15 – Income Taxes
The Company determines its income taxes for interim reporting periods by applying the Company’s estimated annual effective tax rate to the year-to-date results, adjusted for tax items discrete to each period.
Continuing Operations
For the three and nine months ended September 26, 2026, the Company recorded an income tax benefit from continuing operations of $555 million and $65 million representing an effective tax rate of (29.6)% and (1.1)%, respectively. The tax benefit for both periods reflected the income tax benefit from foreign-derived deduction eligible income (FDDEI) and research and development (R&D) tax credits. In addition, the tax benefit for the three and nine months ended September 26, 2026 also included a discrete tax benefit related to stock-based compensation of $772 million and $918 million, respectively.
For the three and nine months ended September 27, 2025, the Company recorded an income tax provision from continuing operations of $153 million and an income tax benefit of $558 million representing an effective tax rate of 11.5% and (26.7)%, respectively. The tax provision (benefit) for both periods reflected the income tax benefit from foreign-derived intangible income (FDII) and R&D tax credits, partially offset by the tax rate detriment from foreign earnings, as well as a discrete tax benefit related to stock-based compensation. In addition, the tax benefit for the nine months ended September 27, 2025 also included a discrete tax benefit of $853 million related to the release of uncertain tax positions pertaining to the reasonable cause relief for dual consolidated losses approved by the Internal Revenue Service (IRS) in April 2025.
As of September 26, 2026 and December 27, 2025, the Company had long-term income tax liabilities related to unrecognized tax benefits, which included interest and penalties, of $905 million and $806 million, respectively, recorded under Other long-term liabilities in the Company’s Consolidated Balance Sheets.

Overview and Recent Developments
We are a global semiconductor company primarily offering:
•Artificial Intelligence (AI) accelerators, microprocessors (CPUs) for servers, graphics processing units (GPUs), accelerated processing units (APUs), data processing units (DPUs), AI Network Interface Cards (AI NICs), Field Programmable Gate Arrays (FPGAs) and adaptive System-on-Chip (SoC) products for data centers;
•CPUs, APUs, chipsets for desktops and notebooks, discrete GPUs, semi-custom SoC products and development services; and
•embedded CPUs, APUs, FPGAs, System on Modules (SOMs), and adaptive SoC products.
From time to time, we may also sell or license portions of our intellectual property (IP) portfolio.
In this section, we will describe the general financial condition and the results of operations of Advanced Micro Devices, Inc. and its wholly-owned subsidiaries (collectively, “we”, “us,” “our”, “AMD” or the “Company”), including a discussion of our results of operations for the three and nine months ended September 26, 2026 compared to the prior year period and an analysis of changes in our financial condition.
Net revenue for the three months ended September 26, 2026 was $13.4 billion, a 45% increase compared to the prior year period. The increase in net revenue was driven by an increase in Data Center segment revenue primarily driven by the strong demand for our AMD EPYC™ processors and the launch of our AMD Instinct™ MI450 Series GPUs, and an increase in Embedded segment revenue as demand strengthened across end markets, partially offset by a decrease in our Client and Gaming segment revenue primarily due to lower demand for semi-custom products.
Gross margin for the three months ended September 26, 2026 was 54% compared to gross margin of 52% for the prior year period, a 2 percentage point increase primarily driven by a favorable product mix, including higher Data Center segment revenue.
Operating income for the three months ended September 26, 2026 was $2.8 billion compared to operating income of $1.3 billion for the prior year period. The increase in operating income was due to higher gross profit, partially offset by higher operating expenses. Net income for the three months ended September 26, 2026 was $2.4 billion compared to net income of $1.2 billion for the prior year period. The increase in net income was primarily driven by higher operating income.
As of September 26, 2026, our cash, cash equivalents and short-term investments were $15.8 billion compared to $10.6 billion as of December 27, 2025. During the nine months ended September 26, 2026, we generated $6.5 billion of cash from operating activities and we returned $221 million to stockholders through the repurchase of common stock under our stock repurchase program (Repurchase Program).
26
Table of Contents
In August and September 2026, we entered into definitive agreements to acquire Taalas Inc. and World Labs Technologies, Inc. to strengthen our model research and inference capabilities and development of future super intelligence (SI) hardware, software and systems. The acquisitions are expected to close in the fourth quarter of fiscal year 2026, subject to regulatory approvals and customary closing conditions.
In August 2026, we issued $4.75 billion in aggregate principal amount of senior notes, with proceeds intended for general corporate purposes, including the repayment of debt. We also repaid in full the $875 million aggregate principal amount of our 4.212% Senior Notes as the notes matured in September 2026.
We have entered and may continue to enter into commercial arrangements to support our operations and anticipated growth, as well as the development of the broader SI infrastructure ecosystem by customers and commercial partners and expand access to our AI technologies. These arrangements include long-term capacity purchase agreements, cloud compute capacity commitments, leases (including leases for general corporate purposes and data center leases), lease guarantees and other credit support and financing arrangements. Under our cloud compute capacity arrangements, we have committed, and expect to continue to commit in the future, to purchase GPU compute capacity from cloud service providers (CSPs) either to the extent it is not successfully placed with third-party customers, or for capacity we reserved and may sell or assign to third parties. We entered into data center leases which we expect to assign or sublease to commercial partners, and provided credit support to certain commercial partners in the form of guarantees of their lease and debt obligations in the event of their default. See Note 8 - Financial Instruments, Note 10 - Leases and Note 11 - Commitments and Contingencies in Part 1, Item 1 of this Form 10-Q.
We intend the discussion of our financial condition and results of operations that follows to provide information that will assist in understanding our financial statements, the changes in certain key items in those financial statements from period to period, the primary factors that resulted in those changes, and how certain accounting principles, policies and estimates affect our financial statements.
Critical Accounting Policies and Estimates
Our discussion and analysis of our financial condition and results of operations are based upon our condensed consolidated financial statements, which have been prepared in accordance with U.S. generally accepted accounting principles (U.S. GAAP). The preparation of our financial statements requires us to make estimates and judgments that affect the reported amounts in our consolidated financial statements. We evaluate our estimates on an ongoing basis, including those related to our revenue, inventories, goodwill, long-lived and intangible assets, business combination accounting, warrants issued to customers and income taxes. We base our estimates on historical experience and on various other assumptions that we believe to be reasonable under the circumstances, the results of which form the basis for making judgments about the carrying values of assets and liabilities. Although actual results have historically been reasonably consistent with management’s expectations, the actual results may differ from these estimates or our estimates may be affected by different assumptions or conditions.
Warrants Issued to Customers. Fair value is based on the estimated probability of the customers meeting the technical and commercial conditions required for exercisability and on market-based assumptions. The estimate of the probability of meeting the technical and commercial conditions by the customers requires significant judgment in evaluating commercial, technical, market and third-party execution considerations. Market-based assumptions include our stock price, Monte Carlo simulation assumptions such as expected volatility, and historical correlation of our stock price returns with a market index. The warrant liability is dependent on our estimate of customers' total probable purchases which requires judgment.
There were no other significant changes for the three and nine months ended September 26, 2026 to the items that we disclosed as our critical accounting estimates in the Management’s Discussion and Analysis of Financial Condition and Results of Operations section of our Annual Report on Form 10-K for the fiscal year ended December 27, 2025.

Data Center
Data Center net revenue of $8.5 billion for the three months ended September 26, 2026 increased by 97%, compared to net revenue of $4.3 billion for the prior year period. Data Center net revenue of $21.0 billion for the nine months ended September 26, 2026 increased by 87%, compared to net revenue of $11.3 billion for the prior year period. The increase in both periods was primarily driven by strong demand for our AMD EPYC processors and AMD Instinct GPUs.
Data Center operating income was $2.9 billion for the three months ended September 26, 2026, compared to operating income of $1.1 billion for the prior year period. Data Center operating income was $6.6 billion for the nine months ended September 26, 2026, compared to operating income of $1.9 billion for the prior year period. The increase in operating income in both periods was primarily driven by higher revenue, partially offset by higher cost of sales and operating expenses.

Client and Gaming
Client and Gaming net revenue of $3.7 billion for the three months ended September 26, 2026 decreased by 8%, compared to net revenue of $4.0 billion for the prior year period. Client and Gaming net revenue of $11.2 billion for the nine months ended September 26, 2026 increased by 5%, compared to net revenue of $10.6 billion for the prior year period.
Client net revenue of $2.9 billion for the three months ended September 26, 2026 increased by 7% compared to net revenue of $2.8 billion for the prior year period, primarily driven by a 6% increase in unit shipments of client processors and a 1% increase in average selling price of client processors. Client net revenue of $8.9 billion for the nine months ended September 26, 2026 increased by 18% compared to net revenue of $7.5 billion for the prior year period, primarily driven by a 21% increase in unit shipments of client processors, partially offset by a 2% decrease in average selling price of client processors. The increase in unit shipments in both periods was primarily driven by AMD RyzenTM mobile processors. The decrease in average selling price in the nine months period was primarily due to a shift in mix of Ryzen processor sales, including lower AMD Ryzen desktop processors sales.
Gaming net revenue of $0.8 billion for the three months ended September 26, 2026 decreased by 40% compared to net revenue of $1.3 billion for the prior year period. Gaming net revenue of $2.3 billion for the nine months ended September 26, 2026 decreased by 26% compared to net revenue of $3.1 billion for the prior year period. The decrease in both periods was primarily due to lower semi-custom revenue.
Client and Gaming operating income was $573 million for the three months ended September 26, 2026, compared to operating income of $867 million for the prior year period. The decrease in operating income was primarily due to lower revenue. Client and Gaming operating income was $1.7 billion for the nine months ended September 26, 2026, compared to operating income of $2.1 billion for the prior year period. The decrease in operating income was primarily due to higher cost of sales and operating expenses.
Embedded
Embedded net revenue of $1.1 billion for the three months ended September 26, 2026 increased by 29%, compared to net revenue of $857 million for the prior year period. Embedded net revenue of $3.0 billion for the nine months ended September 26, 2026 increased by 18%, compared to net revenue of $2.5 billion for the prior year period. Net revenue increased in both periods as demand strengthened across end markets.
Embedded operating income was $453 million for the three months ended September 26, 2026, compared to operating income of $283 million for the prior year period. Embedded operating income was $1.2 billion for the nine months ended September 26, 2026, compared to operating income of $886 million for the prior year period. The increase in operating income in both periods was primarily driven by higher revenue, partially offset by higher cost of sales and operating expenses.
All Other
All Other operating loss of $1.1 billion for the three months ended September 26, 2026 increased by 13% compared to All Other operating loss of $954 million for the prior year period. All Other operating loss of $3.2 billion for the nine months ended September 26, 2026 increased by 9%, compared to All Other operating loss of $2.9 billion for the prior year period. The increase in both periods was primarily due to higher stock-based compensation expense as a result of higher headcount, partially offset by a lower amortization of acquisition-related intangibles.
International Sales
International sales, based on billing location of customers who purchased directly from us, were 57% and 64% of net revenue for the three months ended September 26, 2026 and September 27, 2025, respectively, and 66% and 67% of net revenue for the nine months ended September 26, 2026 and September 27, 2025, respectively. We expect that international sales will continue to be a significant portion of total sales in the foreseeable future. Substantially all of our sales transactions were denominated in U.S. dollars.

R&D expenses of $7.7 billion for the nine months ended September 26, 2026 increased by $2.0 billion, or 34%, compared to $5.8 billion for the prior year period. MG&A expenses of $1.3 billion for the three months ended September 26, 2026 increased by $0.2 billion, or 21%, compared to $1.1 billion for the prior year period. MG&A expenses of $4.0 billion for the nine months ended September 26, 2026 increased by $1.0 billion, or 34%, compared to $2.9 billion for the prior year period. The increase in both periods was primarily due to an increase in go‑to‑market activities to support our revenue growth.

The decrease in both periods was primarily due to certain acquisition-related intangibles that were fully amortized in the prior fiscal year.

Other income (expense), net primarily consists of change in fair value of warrant liability, interest income, gains and losses from investments, and foreign currency transaction gains and losses.

Other income (expense), net for the nine months ended September 26, 2026 was $(139) million, a decrease of $358 million, compared to $219 million for the prior year period. The decrease in both periods was primarily driven by the change in fair value of warrant liability of $1.1 billion.
Income Taxes
We determine income taxes for interim reporting periods by applying our estimated annual effective tax rate to the year-to-date results and adjusted for tax items discrete to each period.
For the three and nine months ended September 26, 2026, we recorded an income tax benefit from continuing operations of $555 million and $65 million representing an effective tax rate of (29.6)% and (1.1)%, respectively. The tax benefit for both periods reflected the income tax benefit from foreign-derived deduction eligible income (FDDEI) and R&D tax credits. In addition, the tax benefit for the three and nine months ended September 26, 2026 also included a discrete tax benefit related to stock-based compensation of $772 million and $918 million, respectively.
For the three and nine months ended September 27, 2025, we recorded an income tax provision from continuing operations of $153 million and an income tax benefit of $558 million representing an effective tax rate of 11.5% and (26.7)%, respectively. The tax provision (benefit) for both periods reflected the income tax benefit from foreign-derived intangible income (FDII) and R&D tax credits, partially offset by the tax rate detriment from foreign earnings, as well as a discrete tax benefit related to stock-based compensation. In addition, the tax benefit for the nine months ended September 27, 2025 also included a discrete tax benefit of $853 million related to the release of uncertain tax positions pertaining to the reasonable cause relief for dual consolidated losses approved by the Internal Revenue Service (IRS) in April 2025.
Results of Discontinued Operations
Net income from discontinued operations for the nine months ended September 26, 2026 of $3 million included tax provision adjustments. Net income from discontinued operations for the three and nine months ended September 27, 2025 of $71 million and $175 million include the results of operations of the ZT Manufacturing Business, that was divested in October 2025. See Note 5 - Acquisitions and Divestitures in Part 1, Item 1 of this Form 10-Q.
FINANCIAL CONDITION
Liquidity and Capital Resources
As of September 26, 2026 and December 27, 2025, our cash, cash equivalents and short-term investments were $15.8 billion and $10.6 billion, respectively. We also have a $5.0 billion unsecured revolving credit facility that expires in 2031 and a commercial paper program under which we may issue up to $5.5 billion of unsecured commercial paper notes with maturities of up to 397 days. We had no borrowings outstanding under either program as of September 26, 2026.
Material Cash Requirements
As of September 26, 2026, the aggregate principal amount of our debt was $7.1 billion, compared to $3.3 billion as of December 27, 2025, reflecting our issuance of senior notes in four tranches with an aggregate principal amount of $4.75 billion in August 2026, partially offset by the repayment of $875 million aggregate principal amount of our 4.212% Senior Notes due 2026 (4.212% Senior Notes) at maturity. Our outstanding debt matures between 2028 and 2052, with none due within the next 12 months.
31
Table of Contents
In addition to our debt obligations, we have entered and may continue to enter into commercial arrangements to support our operations and anticipated growth as well as the development of the broader SI infrastructure ecosystem by our customers and commercial partners and expand access to our AI technologies. These arrangements include long-term capacity purchase agreements, cloud compute capacity commitments, leases (including leases for general corporate purposes and data center leases), lease guarantees and other credit support and financing arrangements.
As of September 26, 2026, unconditional commitments related to our operations were $48.8 billion, of which $33.4 billion is expected to be paid in the remainder of fiscal year 2026 and fiscal year 2027. We had future payments of $0.2 billion under leases for our operations that have not yet commenced and investment commitments of up to $5.1 billion. Commitments for cloud compute capacity arrangements that are intended to support the broader SI infrastructure development and expand access to our technologies were $6.2 billion, of which $1.5 billion is expected to be paid in the remainder of fiscal year 2026 and fiscal year 2027. Future payments under data center leases not yet commenced that we expect to assign or sublease to commercial partners were $13.3 billion, and the maximum potential amount of future payments under our guarantees was $8.5 billion. Remaining payments under leases recognized on our condensed consolidated balance sheet, including data center leases related to the development of SI infrastructure, were $2.7 billion. We have subleased certain of these data center leases to commercial partners and expect to receive $0.9 billion under these noncancellable subleases through 2036. In addition to these commitments, we invested $1.0 billion in an equity offering of a public company in October 2026.
Unconditional commitments related to our operations consist primarily of obligations to purchase wafers, substrates, memory and other components and assembly, test, mark and packaging services for manufacturing. It also includes cloud compute capacity used for our internal research and development and various licenses and service agreements. Leases for our operations that have not yet commenced are expected to commence beginning in the first quarter of fiscal year 2027. Our equity investment commitments are subject to certain conditions and are expected to be funded through fiscal year 2028.
Commitments that are intended to support the development of the broader SI infrastructure and expanding access to our technologies consist of cloud compute capacity and data center leases not yet commenced, both of which are made available to third parties, as well as guarantees. Under cloud compute capacity arrangements, we commit to purchase GPU compute capacity from CSPs, either to the extent it is not placed by third-party customers, or for capacity we reserve and may sell or assign to third parties. Accordingly, our commitments represent maximum amounts, which are reduced as third-party customers pay for the capacity. We expect to assign or sublease data center leases not yet commenced to commercial partners. Our guarantees provide credit support for certain commercial partners’ lease and debt obligations and would require payment only in the event of their default. As of September 26, 2026, no commercial partner was in default and no amounts had been paid under these guarantees.
See Note 8 – Financial Instruments, Note 10 – Leases and Note 11 – Commitments and Contingencies in Part 1, Item 1 of this Form 10-Q for additional information.
We expect to fund our operations, capital expenditures, commitments and strategic activities over the next 12 months and beyond through our cash, cash equivalents, short-term investments and cash flows from operations, together with borrowing capacity under our revolving credit facility and commercial paper program, which we believe will be sufficient for these needs. We may also access the capital markets to supplement our liquidity.

Operating Activities
Our working capital cash inflows and outflows from operations are primarily cash collections from our customers, payments for inventory purchases and payments for employee-related expenditures.
Net cash provided by operating activities of continuing operations was $6.5 billion in the nine months ended September 26, 2026, primarily due to our net income of $6.1 billion, adjusted for non-cash and non-operating charges of $4.0 billion and net cash outflows of $3.6 billion from changes in our operating assets and liabilities. The primary drivers of the changes in operating assets and liabilities included a $3.5 billion increase in inventory primarily to support the continued ramp of Data Center products in advanced process technology nodes, $2.9 billion increase in accounts receivable driven primarily by higher revenue, a $2.1 billion increase in prepaid expenses and other assets primarily due to prepayments of supply agreements, partially offset by a $4.5 billion increase in accounts payable primarily due to timing of payment obligations and higher inventory purchases.
Net cash provided by operating activities of continuing operations was $4.2 billion in the nine months ended September 27, 2025, primarily due to our net income of $2.6 billion, adjusted for non-cash and non-operating charges of $3.4 billion and net cash outflows of $1.8 billion from changes in our operating assets and liabilities. The primary driver of the change in operating assets and liabilities was a $1.6 billion increase in inventory primarily to support the continued ramp of Data Center products in advanced process technology nodes. Net cash provided by operating activities of the ZT Manufacturing Business, classified as discontinued operations, was $920 million.

Net cash used in investing activities of the ZT Manufacturing Business, classified as discontinued operations, was $30 million due to purchases of equipment.

== controls ==
ITEM 4. CONTROLS AND PROCEDURES
We maintain disclosure controls and procedures that are designed to ensure that information required to be disclosed in our reports made under the Securities Exchange Act of 1934, as amended, is recorded, processed, summarized and reported within the time periods specified in SEC rules and forms, and that such information is accumulated and communicated to our management, including our Chief Executive Officer (CEO) and Chief Financial Officer (CFO) as appropriate, to allow for timely decisions regarding required disclosure. In designing and evaluating our disclosure controls and procedures, our management recognizes that any controls and procedures, no matter how well designed and operated, can provide only reasonable assurance of achieving the desired control objectives, and our management is required to apply its judgment in evaluating the cost-benefit relationship of possible controls and procedures.
As of September 26, 2026, the end of the period covered by this report, we carried out an evaluation under the supervision and with the participation of our management, including our CEO and CFO, of the effectiveness of the design and operation of our disclosure controls and procedures. Based on the foregoing, our CEO and CFO concluded that our disclosure controls and procedures were effective at the reasonable assurance level.
There were no changes in our internal controls over financial reporting for the three months ended September 26, 2026 that materially affected, or are reasonably likely to materially affect, our internal controls over financial reporting.

== risk_factors ==
ITEM 1A. RISK FACTORS
The risks and uncertainties described below are not the only ones we face. If any of the following risks actually occurs, our business, financial condition or results of operations could be materially adversely affected. In addition, you should consider the interrelationship and compounding effects of two or more risks occurring simultaneously.
Risk Factors Summary
The following is a summary of the principal risks that could adversely affect our business, financial condition and results of operations.
Economic and Strategic Risks
•The markets in which our products are sold are highly competitive and rapidly evolving.
•The semiconductor industry is highly cyclical and has experienced severe downturns.
•The demand for our products depends in part on the market conditions in the industries into which they are sold.
•The success of our business depends on our ability to introduce products on a timely basis with features and performance levels that provide value to our customers while supporting significant industry transitions.
•The loss of a significant customer may have a material adverse effect on us.
•Economic and market uncertainty may adversely impact our business and operating results.
•Our operating results are subject to quarterly and seasonal sales patterns.
•If we cannot adequately protect our technology or other intellectual property through patents, copyrights, trade secrets, trademarks and other measures, we may lose a competitive advantage and incur significant expenses.
•Unfavorable currency exchange rate fluctuations could adversely affect us.
Operational and Technology Risks
•We rely on third parties to manufacture our products, and if they are unable to do so on a timely basis in sufficient quantities and using competitive technologies, our business could be materially adversely affected.
•Essential equipment, materials, substrates or manufacturing processes may not be available to us.
•We may fail to achieve expected manufacturing yields for our products.
•Our revenue from our semi-custom System-on-Chip (SoC) products is dependent upon our semi-custom SoC products being incorporated into customers’ products and the success of those products.
•Our products may be subject to security vulnerabilities that could have a material adverse effect on us.
•IT outages, data loss, data breaches and cyberattacks could disrupt operations and compromise our intellectual property or other sensitive information be costly to remediate or cause significant damage to our business, reputation, financial condition and results of operations.
•Uncertainties involving the ordering and shipment of our products could materially adversely affect us.
•Our ability to design and introduce new products depends on the use of third-party intellectual property.
•We depend on third-party companies for the design, manufacture and supply of motherboards, software, memory and other computer platform components to support our business and products.

•If we lose Microsoft Corporation’s support for our products or other software vendors do not design and develop software to run on our products, our ability to sell our products could be materially adversely affected.
•Our reliance on third-party distributors and add-in-board (AIB) partners subjects us to certain risks.
•Our business depends on the proper functioning of our internal business processes and information systems.
•Our products may not be compatible with some or all industry-standard software and hardware.
•Costs related to defective products could have a material adverse effect on us.
•We may fail to maintain the efficiency of our supply chain as we respond to changes in customer demand.
•We outsource to third parties certain supply-chain logistics functions.

•Evolving expectations from governments, investors, customers and other stakeholders regarding corporate responsibility matters could result in additional costs, harm to our reputation and a loss of customers.

General Risks
•Our worldwide operations are subject to political, legal and economic risks and natural disasters.
•We may incur future impairments of our technology license purchases.
•If we are unable to attract, develop and retain key employees, our business could be materially affected.
•Our stock price is subject to volatility.
For a more complete discussion of the material risks facing our business, see below.
36
Table of Contents
Economic and Strategic Risks
The markets in which our products are sold are highly competitive and rapidly evolving.
Delivering the latest and best products to market on time is critical to revenue growth. The competitiveness of our products depends on a number of factors, including: performance, total cost of ownership, timely product introductions, product quality and reliability, product features and capabilities, energy efficiency (including power consumption and battery life, given their impact on total cost of ownership), size (or form factor), selling price, cost, adherence to industry standards (and the creation of open industry standards), level of integration, software and hardware compatibility, ease of use and functionality of software design tools, completeness of applicable software solutions, security and stability, brand recognition and availability.
Competition is expected to remain intense, driven by rapid technological change, evolving standards, shifting customer preferences, product obsolescence, and frequent product launches from both established and new competitors. Some of our competitors may possess stronger market positions, larger customer bases, more design wins, and greater financial, sales, marketing, and distribution resources than us. As a result, they may be able to acquire market share or limit our ability to do so, more effectively capitalize on new market opportunities, and transition their products more efficiently than we can. Some competitors are pursuing alternative computing architectures, such as Arm, which could grow the Arm ecosystem and increase competition in consumer, commercial and data center markets, reducing demand for our products. Additionally, we may encounter competition from customers who internally develop products to support super intelligence (SI) workloads similar to those supported by our products, particularly as SI continues to advance and become integrated into the markets in which we compete. To the extent our customers develop products that compete with ours, including custom AI accelerators, and replace or reduce their reliance on our products, our competitive position may be weakened and may limit our ability to grow or maintain our market share.
Our competitors may use their market position and financial resources to market and price their products in a way that dissuades customers from purchasing from us. For example, Intel Corporation (Intel) uses its microprocessor market position to price its products aggressively and target our customers and channel partners with special incentives. These aggressive activities have reduced and may reduce our unit sales and average selling prices for many of our products, adversely affecting our business. Similarly, Nvidia Corporation (Nvidia) leverages its market position in the data center GPU market, financial resources, and proprietary software ecosystem to promote its systems and influence customers that do business with us. Our competitors’ business practices, including allocation strategies, pricing actions, product mix and introduction schedules, licensing terms, marketing arrangements, product bundling strategies, lack of software interoperability and business acquisitions and investments can limit customers’ ability to choose alternative products, including ours. This may limit our market share and decrease our margins and profitability, which may have a material adverse effect on our business.
In addition, strategic partnerships, acquisitions and business collaborations by and between our competitors may increase competition and adversely affect our business. For example, in September 2025, Nvidia announced a partnership and investment in Intel to partner on new data center and client platform products. This partnership may result in increased competition and pricing pressure for our products or could prevent us from participating in other opportunities, which could materially adversely affect our business, financial condition and margins.
The semiconductor industry is highly cyclical and has experienced severe downturns that have materially adversely affected, and may continue to materially adversely affect, our business in the future.
The semiconductor industry is highly cyclical and has experienced significant downturns, often alongside constant and rapid technological change, wide fluctuations in supply and demand, continuous new product introductions, price erosion and declines in general economic conditions. The growth of SI is further creating pressure on the semiconductor industry to timely design, manufacture and deliver semiconductor products and solutions to meet customer demand for computing power and SI infrastructure. We have incurred substantial losses in previous downturns, due to substantial declines in average selling prices; the cyclical nature of supply and demand imbalances in the semiconductor industry; a decline in demand for end-user products that incorporate our products; and excess inventory levels and periods of inventory adjustment. Such industry-wide fluctuations may materially adversely affect us in the future. Global economic uncertainty and weakness have in the past impacted the semiconductor market as consumers and businesses have deferred purchases, which negatively impacted demand for our products. Our financial performance has been, and may in the future be, negatively affected by these downturns.

Table of Contents
The growth of our business is also dependent on continued demand for our products from high-growth adjacent emerging global markets. Our ability to be successful in such markets depends in part on our ability to establish adequate local infrastructure, as well as our ability to cultivate and maintain local relationships in these markets. If demand from these markets is below our expectations, sales of our products may decrease, which would have a material adverse effect on us.
The demand for our products depends in part on the market conditions in the industries into which they are sold. Fluctuations in demand for our products or a market decline in any of these industries could have a material adverse effect on our results of operations.
Industry-wide fluctuations in the computer marketplace have materially adversely affected us in the past and may materially adversely affect us in the future. We offer products that are used in different end markets and the demand for our products can vary among our Data Center, Client, Gaming and Embedded end markets. In our Data Center segment, we offer products that are designed to be optimized for generative AI applications and since 2024, we have experienced significant demand for our accelerators. The demand for such products will in part depend on the extent to which our customers utilize generative AI solutions in a wide variety of applications, and both the near-term and long-term trajectory of such generative AI solutions is unknown. Data center deployments may occur slower than our customers or the market anticipate. Customers may be unable to secure sufficient data center capacity and may face power and water constraints, industry-wide memory shortage and related rising prices and other supply chain bottlenecks, unfavorable developments in public perception or evolving laws and regulations related to data centers that can cause construction delays in the scheduled buildout of data centers. Since our data center business depends in part on the timing and scale of our customers’ SI infrastructure deployments, any delay or reduction in buildout resulting from these factors could impact the timing of customer adoption of our products, impede our growth strategy and could have a material adverse effect on our business, financial conditions and future growth strategy. Customers may also lack, or be unable to secure, capital to fund their required SI infrastructure and may request alternative financing or deferred‑payment arrangements from vendors and suppliers.
Our Client and Gaming segment revenue includes products sold into the consumer desktop and notebook PC segments and will depend in part on the market’s adoption of AI PCs. We are actively building SI capabilities into our Client products, such as Ryzen AI PC processors, but there can be no assurance about the rate and pace of adoption of such product offerings. In the past, revenue from the Client and Gaming segment has experienced a decline driven by, among other factors, the adoption of smaller and other form factors, increased competition and changes in replacement cycles. The success of our semi-custom SoC products in our Client and Gaming segment is dependent on securing customers for our semi-custom design pipeline and consumer market conditions, including the success of game console systems and next generation consoles for Sony and Microsoft. Semi-custom sales can be cyclical, and may decrease over time if our customers decide to not introduce new products.
Our Embedded segment primarily includes embedded CPUs, APUs, FPGAs, System on Modules (SOMs) and adaptive SoC products some of which are subject to macroeconomic trends, geopolitical volatility and volatile business conditions. To the extent our embedded customers are faced with higher inventory levels, they may choose to draw down their existing inventory and order less of our products.
The success of our business depends on our ability to introduce products on a timely basis with features and performance levels that provide value to our customers while supporting and coinciding with significant industry transitions.
Our success depends to a significant extent on the development, qualification, implementation and acceptance of new product designs and improvements that provide value to our customers. Our ability to identify industry changes, and adapt our strategy to develop, qualify and distribute, and have manufactured, new products and related technologies to meet evolving industry trends and requirements, at prices acceptable to our customers and on a timely basis, are significant factors in determining our competitiveness in our target markets. We cannot assure you that we will be able to meet the evolving needs of industry changes or that our efforts to execute our product roadmap will result in innovative products and technologies that provide value to our customers. If we fail to or are delayed in identifying, developing, qualifying or shipping new products or technologies that provide value to our customers and address these new trends, or if we fail to predict which new form factors, product features preferences or requirements consumers will adopt and adapt our business accordingly, we may lose out on design wins and lose competitive positioning, which could cause us to lose market share.

Although we make substantial investments in research and development, we cannot be certain that we will be able to develop, obtain or successfully implement new products and technologies on a timely basis or that they will be well-received by our customers. Our investments in new products and technologies involve certain risks and uncertainties and could disrupt our ongoing business. Failure to successfully develop increasingly advanced technologies, including our portfolio of hardware products and software tools, or monetize our products, could impact our revenue and we may incur unanticipated liabilities. We cannot be certain that our ongoing investments in new products and technologies will be successful and will result in adoption of these product offerings at the expected rate or pace or at all. For example, as part of our pervasive AI strategy, we have a portfolio of hardware products and software tools to allow our customers to develop scalable AI solutions. We are actively building AI capabilities into our products, but there can be no assurance about the rate and pace of adoption of such product offerings. In our Data Center segment, we offer products that are designed to be optimized for generative AI applications and we have experienced significant demand for our accelerators. The demand for such products in part will depend on the extent to which our customers utilize generative AI solutions in a wide variety of applications as both the near-term and long-term trajectory of such generative AI solutions is unknown. If we fail to develop and timely offer or deploy such products and technologies, keep pace with the product offerings of our competitors, or adapt to unexpected changes in industry standards or disruptive technological innovation, our business could be adversely affected. Additionally, our efforts in developing new SI technology solutions are inherently risky and may not always succeed. If we are unable to execute on new products or business strategies, we may incur significant costs, resources, investments and delays and not achieve a return on investment or capitalize on the opportunities presented by demand for AI solutions. Moreover, while SI adoption is likely to continue and may accelerate, the long-term trajectory of this technological trend is uncertain.
Delays in developing, qualifying or shipping new products may cause us to miss our customers’ product design windows or, in some cases, breach contractual obligations. If our products are not selected by our customers in the initial design of their computer systems or products, they are typically excluded until at least the next design cycle. The process of being qualified for inclusion in a customer’s system or product can be lengthy and could cause us to further miss a cycle in the demand of end-users, which could result in a loss of market share and harm our business. We also depend on the timing and success of our customers’ platform launches. If our customers delay their product launches or if our customers do not effectively market their platforms with our products, it could result in a delay in bringing our products to market and cause us to miss a cycle in the demand of end-users, which could materially adversely affect our business. In addition, as market demand requires that products incorporate new features and performance standards on an industry-wide basis, product pricing declines over the life of a specific product. The introduction of new products and enhancements to existing products is necessary to maintain the overall corporate average selling price. If we are unable to introduce new products with sufficiently high sale prices or to increase unit sales volumes capable of offsetting the reductions in the sale prices of existing products over time, our business could be materially adversely affected.
Product transition risks may increase as the computing industry shifts toward shorter launch cycles and a broader range of accelerated computing platforms. Product transitions are complex and as such we may ship both new and prior-generation products concurrently. Customer adoption patterns can vary and while some customers may shift to newer products more quickly and reduce demand for current-generation offerings, other customers may lower their inventory of existing products before purchasing new ones.

The increased frequency of product transitions and expansion of our product portfolio including the introduction of system-level architectures, such as the Helios rack-scale solutions, which we develop and license for implementation by customers and their manufacturing partners heighten the challenges of managing our supply and demand and could adversely affect our revenue and inventory management. Although we supply certain products used in Helios rack-scale solutions, we do not manufacture or sell the completed Helios rack-scale solutions. The increasing frequency and complexity of newly introduced products may also result in unanticipated quality or production issues that could result in product delays. Helios rack-scale solutions also incorporate components that we do not design, manufacture or supply, including networking, memory, power, cooling, mechanical and other components, which our customers and their manufacturing partners source from third-party suppliers. If any of these third-party components are in short supply, subject to extended lead times or quality issues, or otherwise unavailable on acceptable terms, the manufacture, delivery and deployment of Helios rack-scale solutions could be delayed or limited. This could delay or reduce demand for our products used in those systems, affect the timing of our revenue, result in excess inventory or inventory write-downs, and adversely affect our business, financial condition and results of operations. In addition, we have elected and may in the future elect to support or commit to the procurement of certain third-party components to help secure supply for Helios rack-scale solutions, which could expose us to additional costs, inventory risk and other financial obligations if demand does not materialize as expected or if deployments are delayed.
The loss of a significant customer may have a material adverse effect on us.
We depend on a small number of customers for a substantial portion of our business and we expect that a small number of customers will continue to account for a significant part of our revenue and receivables in the future. If one of our key customers decides to stop buying our products, materially reduces its operations or its demand for our products, or has operations that are materially impaired for a significant period of time such that it is unable to receive or utilize our products, or pay its liabilities, our business would be materially adversely affected.
Economic and market uncertainty may adversely impact our business and operating results.
Uncertain global or regional economic conditions have and may in the future adversely impact our business. Uncertainty in the economic environment or other unfavorable changes in economic conditions, such as inflation, fluctuating interest rates, recession, slowing growth, increased unemployment, tighter credit markets, changes or uncertainty in fiscal monetary or trade policy, implementation of new or increased tariffs, retaliatory tariffs by other countries or other trade restrictions, or currency fluctuations, may negatively impact consumer confidence and spending causing our customers to stop or postpone purchases. During challenging economic times, our current or potential future customers may experience cash flow problems and as a result may modify, delay or cancel plans to purchase our products. Additionally, if our customers are not successful in generating sufficient revenue or are unable to secure financing, they may not be able to pay, or may delay payment of, accounts receivable that they owe us. If current or prospective customers default on or delay payments to us, our earnings and cash flow could be adversely impacted. This risk is heightened as we expect that a small number of customers will continue to account for a substantial part of our revenue. Moreover, our key suppliers may reduce their output or become insolvent, thereby adversely impacting our ability to manufacture our products. Adverse changes in economic conditions could increase costs of memory, equipment, materials or substrates and other supply chain expenses. For example, there is currently an industry-wide memory shortage as the demand for such components has outpaced supply. The price of memory has also increased as a result of the shortage. If we are not able to procure a stable supply of materials, including memory, on an ongoing basis and at reasonable costs to meet our production requirements, we could experience a supply shortage or an increase in production costs, which could negatively impact our gross margin and materially adversely affect our business. Our ability to forecast our operating results, make business decisions and execute our business strategy could be adversely impacted by challenging macroeconomic conditions. In addition, uncertain economic conditions could lead to higher borrowing costs and reduced availability of capital and credit markets, making it more difficult for us to raise funds through borrowings or private or public sales of debt or equity securities. An economic downturn or increased uncertainty could also lead to failures of counterparties including financial institutions and insurers, asset impairments and declines in the value of our financial instruments. If a banking institution in which we hold funds fails or is subject to significant adverse conditions in the financial or credit markets, we could be subject to a risk of loss of all or a portion of such uninsured funds or be subject to a delay in accessing all or a portion of such uninsured funds, which in turn could adversely impact our short-term liquidity and ability to meet our operating expense obligations.
